. Hence, we can compute (
4.10
) by summing the residues and find the desired expression using (
2.5
).
∎

Furthermore,
F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)
is invariant under an S-duality-like transformation. Indeed, let us define

W^{\rm NS}_{\mathrm{np}}(\epsilon,t)=F^{\rm NS}_{\rm np}\left(\epsilon,t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{2}\right).

Then we have

Proposition 4.5
.

Under the change of variables

\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\epsilon},\qquad t^{\prime}=\frac{2\pi t}{\epsilon},

we have

W^{\rm NS}_{\rm np}(\epsilon^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\check{\epsilon}}W^{\rm NS}_{\rm np}(\epsilon,t).

(4.12)

Proof.

Equation (
4.12
) follows directly from the integral equation (
4.10
).
∎

Moreover,

Proposition 4.6
.

F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t)
satisfies the difference equation

F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t+\check{\epsilon}/2)-F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=-\mathrm{i}{\rm Li}_{2}(Q).

(4.13)

F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)
also obeys this equation, and moreover satisfies the additional difference equation

F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t+1)-F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+\check{\epsilon}/2)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

(4.14)

Note that the latter difference equation is invisible to its perturbative expansion.

Proof.

The first part follows from an explicit computation using the formal expansion (
2.6
):

\displaystyle F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t+\check{\epsilon}/2)-F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{n}{\rm Li}_{3}(Q)\frac{B_{n}}{n!}\check{\epsilon}^{n-1}\big((-1)^{n-1}+1\big),

\displaystyle=\frac{2B_{1}}{2\pi}\partial_{t}\mathrm{Li}_{3}(Q)=-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q),

where we used that
B_{2n+1}=0
for
n>0
.

For the second part of the proposition, we use the integral representation (
4.10
) and note that

\displaystyle F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t+\check{\epsilon}/2)-F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm e}^{ts}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}=(2\pi)^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{i}{\rm e}^{ts}}{s^{2}}\Big|_{s=2\pi\mathrm{i}k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kt}}{k^{2}}=-\mathrm{i}{\rm Li}_{2}\left(Q\right),

where we have computed the integral by the sum over the residues in the upper half plane.

Similarly, we find

\displaystyle F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t+1)-F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm e}^{\left(t+\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{2}\right)s}}{({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}=\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\check{\epsilon}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{i}{\rm e}^{(t+\check{\epsilon}/2)s}}{s^{2}}\Big|_{s=2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k(t+\check{\epsilon}/2)/\check{\epsilon}}}{k^{2}}=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+\check{\epsilon}/2)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

∎

4.4
Unrefined topological string

The limit in which the parameters of the refined topological string are set to
\epsilon_{1}=-\epsilon_{2}=\lambda
corresponds to the unrefined topological string. The difference equation analogous to (
4.1
) was derived from the asymptotic expansion of the Gromov–Witten potential of the resolved conifold in
[
6
]
following methods of
[
68
]
. It is given by

F^{\rm top}\big(\lambda,t+\check{\lambda}\big)+F^{\rm top}\big(\lambda,t-\check{\lambda}\big)-2F^{\rm top}\left(\lambda,t\right)=-{\rm Li}_{1}(Q),\qquad\check{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda}{2\pi}.

Furthermore a solution in terms of the triple sine function of this difference equation was considered, which was given by
[
8
]

\displaystyle F^{\rm top}_{{\rm np}}(\lambda,t):=\left(\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{6}B_{3,3}\left(t+\check{\lambda}|\check{\lambda},\check{\lambda},1\right)\right)+\log\big(\sin_{3}\big(t+\check{\lambda}|\check{\lambda},\check{\lambda},1\big)\big).

The non-perturbative content of this solution was analyzed in
[
7
]
and in
[
9
]
. It was identified as the Borel summation of the asymptotic series along a distinguished ray on the real axis in the Borel plane.

Here we add a further difference equation, which is the unrefined limit of the third difference equation in (
4.4
). Since the shift is integral, this difference equation is invisible to the periodic asymptotic series. We have that:

Proposition 4.7
.

F^{\rm top}_{{\rm np}}(\lambda,t)
satisfies the difference equation

\displaystyle F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm top}(\lambda,t+1)-F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm top}(\lambda,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\check{\lambda}}\big(\check{\lambda}{\rm Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t/\check{\lambda}}\big)\big).

(4.15)

Proof.

To prove this proposition we take the third difference equation in (
4.4
) and set
\epsilon_{1}=-\epsilon_{2}=\lambda
. The right-hand side is then given in terms of the quantum dilogarithm with repeated argument, for which we can use the expression (
B.8
) in Appendix
B.2
. That is,

-\log\big(\mathcal{S}_{2}\big(t+\check{\lambda}|\check{\lambda},\check{\lambda}\big)\big)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\check{\lambda}}\big(\check{\lambda}{\rm Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t/\check{\lambda}}\big)\big).

∎

Remark 4.8
.

The right-hand side of the difference equation (
4.15
) equals Stokes jump of the Borel resummation of
F^{\textrm{top}}(\lambda,t)
obtained in
[
9
]
. The difference equation can be given the interpretation as a relation between the Borel resummations in the different Stokes sectors. Indeed, as was shown in
[
9
, Corollary 3.12]
,

F^{\rm top}_{{\rho}_{k-n}}(\lambda,t+n)=F_{\rho_{k}}^{\rm top}(\lambda,t),

where
F_{\rho}^{\rm top}
denotes the Borel sum along the ray
\rho
, and
\rho_{k}
is a ray between
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
, where
l_{k}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)
. This relation together with the jumps (
1.1
) is equivalent to the difference equation (
4.15
). We will find a similar interpretations for the analogous difference equation in the NS limit.

4.5
Relations between free energies through difference equations

In this section we derive further relations between the refined topological string free energy and its various limits. These will be useful in the rest of the paper.

Proposition 4.9
.

The two difference equations

\displaystyle F^{{\rm ref}}\left(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}+\check{\epsilon}_{1}\right)-F^{{\rm ref}}\left(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}F^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}\right),

\displaystyle F^{{\rm ref}}\left(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}\right)-F^{{\rm ref}}\left(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}F^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\epsilon_{1},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}}{2}\right)

(4.16)

give a relation between
F^{{\rm ref}}
and
F^{\mathrm{NS}}
.

Proof.

This proposition can again be verified by an explicit computation. We prove the first equation here, the second one follows analogously. Since the above shifts of
F^{\rm ref}
can all be expanded in
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
with
Q
-dependent coefficients as in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), we find

\displaystyle F^{{\rm ref}}\left(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t+\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}}{2}-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}\right)+F^{{\rm ref}}\left(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}}{2}-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}{\rm Li}_{3-m-n}(Q)\frac{B_{m}B_{n}}{m!n!}\mathrm{i}^{m+n}\epsilon_{1}^{m-1}\epsilon_{2}^{n-1}\left((-1)^{m-1}+1\right).

The last factor in the summand only contributes when
m=1
, since all other odd Bernoulli numbers vanish. The right-hand side then becomes

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\rm Li}_{2-n}(Q)\frac{B_{n}}{n!}(\mathrm{i}\epsilon_{2})^{n-1},

which can be matched with the right-hand side of the proposition using the asymptotic expansion (
2.6
).
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

Similarly, the unrefined topological string free energies obey the difference equation

\displaystyle F^{\rm top}\big(\lambda,t+\check{\lambda}\big)-F^{\rm top}(\lambda,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}F^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\lambda,t+\frac{\check{\lambda}}{2}\right).

(4.17)

Remark 4.11
.

•

Similar relations also hold for the free energies with the subscript
{\rm np}
. These can be easily derived from the corresponding integral representations.

•

Equation (
4.17
) is the difference equation relating the topological string free energy to certain Darboux coordinates, as was observed in
[
27
]
and
[
10
]
. This will become clearer in Section
7.5
.

4.6
A quantum curve for closed string moduli

In the following, we interpret the finite difference equations found above as the quantization of an algebraic curve that should be associated with the closed string moduli. The motivation for this is the work of
[
2
]
, in which it was proposed that the mirror curves arising in the mirror constructions of non-compact CY threefolds can be interpreted as the analogs of the Hamiltonians of a quantum mechanical problem. The curve which is quantized in the case of
[
2
]
parametrizes the open string moduli, as was put forward in
[
3
]
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Define

\displaystyle\Psi^{c}_{1}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t):=\frac{Z^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2})}{Z^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}+\check{\epsilon}_{2})},

\displaystyle\Psi^{c}_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t):=\frac{Z^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2})}{Z^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t-\frac{\check{\epsilon}_{1}+\check{\epsilon}_{2}}{2}+\check{\epsilon}_{1})},

where

Z^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)=\exp\big(F^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)\big)
.
Using

Z_{{\rm np}}^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)=\exp\big(F_{{\rm np}}^{{\rm ref}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)\big)

we obtain analogous definitions for
\Psi^{c}_{i,{\rm np}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then the following holds for
i\in\{1,2\}
:

1.

\Psi^{c}_{i}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)
only depends on
\epsilon_{i}
and

\Psi^{c}_{i}(\epsilon_{i},t)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}\left(\epsilon_{i},t-\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{4\pi}\right)\right).

The same is true for
\Psi^{c}_{i,{\rm np}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t)
.

2.

\Psi^{c}_{i}(\epsilon_{i},t)
and
\Psi^{c}_{i,{\rm np}}(\epsilon_{i},t)
satisfy the equation

\big(1-Q-{\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}_{i}\partial_{t}}\big)\Psi^{c}_{i}(\epsilon_{i},t)=0.

3.

We have

\Psi^{c}_{i,{\rm np}}=\mathcal{S}_{2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon}_{i},1)^{-1}.

Proof.

The first part of the proposition follows from (
4.16
), the second part follows by exponentiating the difference equation (
4.1
) and using the definitions. The last point follows, from example, from the integral representation (
4.5
) of
F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm ref}
, the definition of
\Psi_{i,{\rm np}}^{c}
, and the integral representation (
B.6
) of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

We note that the equation satisfied by
\Psi^{c}_{i}
can be interpreted as the quantization of the curve

1-\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}t)-\exp(\mathrm{i}s/2\pi)=0

in
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
,
where the variables
t,s
are promoted to operators
\hat{t},\hat{s}
acting on a Hilbert space and obeying the commutation relations

\left[\hat{t},\hat{s}\right]=\mathrm{i}\epsilon_{i},\qquad i=1,2.

In particular, one can choose a polarization where
\hat{t}
acts as multiplication by
t
and
\hat{s}
acts as
-\mathrm{i}\epsilon_{i}\partial_{t}
.

4.7
Quantum Picard–Fuchs operator

In
[
82
]
the NS limit of Nekrasov’s partition function was studied in the case of Seiberg–Witten theory and it was argued that the NS limit corresponds to a quantization of the classical periods of the Seiberg–Witten differential. These classical periods were computed as explicit period integrals in the original work
[
93
]
and were also obtained as solutions of Picard–Fuchs equations (see
[
76
]
and references therein). A study of a quantum deformation of the Picard–Fuchs equation governing the classical periods was initiated in
[
82
]
. Similar ideas came up in
[
1
]
, where the WKB analysis of quantum curves was used to argue for differential Picard–Fuchs type operators that determine the order by order corrections to the classical periods to obtain the quantum periods. Although the arguments leading to these operators are clear it is perhaps less clear what the interpretation of such an all-order operator should be and how these should be derived systematically. We show in the following that the difference equations which we have studied here provide a clear path towards such all-order operator.

We want to derive a quantum Picard–Fuchs operator which annihilates in particular the quantum period that we expect to be related to the derivative of the topological string free energy in the NS limit. The asymptotic expansion of the latter is

F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\rm Li}_{3-n}(Q)\frac{B_{n}}{n!}(\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{n-1},

as given in (
2.6
). This expansion was shown to satisfy the difference equation (
4.13
)

F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t+\check{\epsilon}/2)-F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=-\mathrm{i}{\rm Li}_{2}(Q).

It follows that
\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
has the asymptotic expansion

\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=2\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\rm Li}_{2-n}(Q)\frac{B_{n}}{n!}(\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{n-1},

and satisfies the difference equation

\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t+\check{\epsilon}/2)-\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=2\pi{\rm Li}_{1}(Q).

Before deriving the quantum Picard–Fuchs operator from this difference equation we would like to include the classical terms discussed in Section
2.5
in this quantum period, so that we in particular reproduce (
2.14
) as the leading contribution of the quantum period in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
. The leading contribution of
\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
is given by

\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{\epsilon}{\rm Li}_{2}(Q)+\mathcal{O}(1).

We would like to modify
F^{\rm NS}
such that the leading piece includes the classical piece of (
2.14
) and hence is of the form

-\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{3}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}{\rm Li}_{2}(Q)\right).

Instead of only adding the term
t^{2}/2
with the correct prefactor, we take advantage of the ambiguity of the linear and constant terms in
t
in the period expansion as well as the ambiguity in the constant term in
\epsilon
in the quantum period, and propose to add the classical term in the guise of a generalized Bernoulli polynomial which satisfies a difference equation on its own. We thus use

B_{2,2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1)=\frac{1}{\check{\epsilon}}\left(t^{2}-t+\frac{1}{6}\right)-t+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{6},

to obtain:

Proposition 4.14
.

Define

\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2):=2\pi^{2}\mathrm{i}\cdot B_{2,2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1)+\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2).

Then we find that:

•

\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
satisfies the difference equation

\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t+\check{\epsilon}/2)-\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=(2\pi)\log\frac{Q}{Q-1}.

(4.18)

•

\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
is a solution of the quantum Picard–Fuchs operator

L_{\epsilon}:=\partial_{t}\circ(1-Q)\circ\partial_{t}\circ\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\check{\epsilon}^{n}}{n!}\partial_{t}^{n}.

Proof.

The difference equation follows from (
4.14
) as well as the difference equation for the generalized Bernoulli polynomial of Proposition
B.1
:

B_{2,2}(t+\check{\epsilon}\mid\check{\epsilon},1)-B_{2,2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1)=2B_{1,1}(t\mid 1)=2t-1.

To show that
\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
is a solution of the quantum Picard–Fuchs operator, we write the difference equation (
4.18
) as:

\big({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}\partial_{t}}-1\big)\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=(2\pi)\log\frac{Q}{Q-1},

expand in
\check{\epsilon}
and further act with
\partial_{t}\circ(1-Q)\circ\partial_{t}
on the result.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

•

We remark that the leading contribution in
\epsilon
to the quantum Picard–Fuchs operator is the so-called extended classical Picard–Fuchs operator
L_{\text{cl}}
, defined as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\circ(1-Q)\circ\partial_{t}\circ\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\check{\epsilon}^{n}}{n!}\partial_{t}^{n}=\check{\epsilon}(\partial_{t}\circ(1-Q)\circ\partial_{t}\circ\partial_{t})+\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{2}\big)=:\check{\epsilon}L_{\text{cl}}+\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{2}\big).

This operator is one order less than the Picard–Fuchs operator given in (
2.13
) and only has the three independent solutions
\varpi^{0}
,
\varpi^{1}
and
\varpi^{2}
of Section
2.5
.

•

The quantum periods
\varpi^{0}_{\epsilon}
and
\varpi^{1}_{\epsilon}
are equal to

\varpi^{0}_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\check{\epsilon}},\qquad\varpi^{1}_{\epsilon}=\frac{t}{\check{\epsilon}},

up to rescaling. This will be further discussed later.

•

We have from Proposition
4.12
(ii) and (iv) that

\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}\left(\epsilon_{i},t-\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{4\pi}\right)\right)=\mathcal{S}_{2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1),

which suggests the non-perturbative extension

\partial_{t}F^{{\rm NS},\sharp}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=2\pi(\pi\mathrm{i}B_{2,2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1)+\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1))).

(4.19)

One can easily check that this is also a solution of the quantum Picard–Fuchs operator
L_{\epsilon}
.

5
Borel sums and Stokes phenomena

In this section we study the Borel summation and associated Stokes phenomena of the following two objects: on one hand we consider the following shift of
F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t)
:

W(\epsilon,t):=F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}tk}}{({\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}k\epsilon}-1)}\qquad\mathrm{with}\quad\check{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon}{2\pi}.

One of the main reasons to study this shifted free energy is that we will be able to relate its Borel sums to the Borel sums and Stokes phenomena of the topological free energy
F^{{\rm top}}(\lambda,t)
studied in
[
9
]
, where
\lambda
denotes the topological string coupling. More specifically, we will see that the Borel sums of the
\epsilon
-expansion of
W(\epsilon,t)
give an
\epsilon
-potential for the Borel sums of the
\lambda
-expansion of
F^{{\rm top}}(\lambda,t)
, provided we set
\lambda=\epsilon
.

Furthermore, we also study the Borel sums and Stokes phenomena of the formal solution
S(\epsilon,x,t)
of the Schrödinger equation (
3.8
), given in (
3.9
); and relate the Borel sums of
S(\epsilon,x,t)
with those of
W(\epsilon,t)
.

Remark 5.1
.

We remark that a similar study of the Borel summability of
S(\epsilon,x,t)
is performed in the recent work
[
52
, Section 4]
. An analogous discussion of the Borel transform of
F^{\rm NS}
is written down in
[
52
, Section 4.5.1]
. We became aware of this work while this paper was being finalized.

5.1
Main results

We will first state the main results, and then prove them in the following subsections. The main results we wish to prove in this section regarding
W(\epsilon,t)
is contained in the following theorem:

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
W(\epsilon,t)
be as before. Then:

•

(Borel sum)
Let
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}-\mathbb{Z}
and consider the rays in the
\epsilon
-plane defined by
l_{k}:=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
l_{\infty}:=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}

(
see Figure
3
on the left
)
. Then given any ray
\rho\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
from
0
to
\infty
different from
\{\pm l_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{\pm l_{\infty}\}
; and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{\rho}
, where
\mathbb{H}_{\rho}
denotes the half-plane centered at
\rho
, the formal
\epsilon
-expansion of
W(\epsilon,t)
is Borel summable with Borel sum:

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t):=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{3}(Q)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)+\frac{\epsilon}{12}\mathrm{Li_{1}}(Q)+\check{\epsilon}\int_{\rho}\mathrm{d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t),

where

\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t+\xi/m}}-\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t-\xi/m}}\right).

In particular, one finds that if
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
and
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
, then for
\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
:

W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t)=F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2),

(5.1)

where
F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t)
was defined in (
4.10
).

•

(Stokes jumps)
Assume that
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
. Furthermore let
\rho
be a ray in the sector determined by the Stokes rays
l_{k+1}
and
l_{k}
, and
\rho^{\prime}
a ray in the sector determined by
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Then for
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{\rho}\cap\mathbb{H}_{\rho^{\prime}}

(
resp.
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{-\rho}\cap\mathbb{H}_{-\rho^{\prime}})
we have

W_{\pm\rho}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\pm\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,t)=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

(5.2)

If
\operatorname{Im}(t)<0
, then the previous jumps also hold provided
\rho
is interchanged with
\rho^{\prime}
in the above formulas.

•

(Limits to
\pm l_{\infty})
Let
\rho_{k}
denote any ray between the Stokes rays
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Furthermore, assume that
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
,
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)>0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)<0
, and
\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
. Then

\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t)

(5.3)

On the other hand, assume that
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
,
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)<0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)<0
,
\operatorname{Re}t<\operatorname{Re}(-\check{\epsilon}+1)
and that
\big|{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t/\check{\epsilon}}\big|<1
. Then

\begin{split}\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t).\end{split}

In other words, the limits to
l_{\infty}
from the right and left give
W(\epsilon,t)
.

The other limits corresponding to
-l_{\infty}
follow from the previous limits and the relations

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)+W_{-\rho}(-\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q).

(5.4)

and

W(\epsilon,t)+W(-\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q).

In particular, the limits to
-l_{\infty}
from the right and left give
W(\epsilon,t)
.

•

(Potential for the Borel sum of the topological free energy)
Given
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, we have the identity

\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)=F_{\rho}(\epsilon,t),

where
F_{\rho}
denotes the Borel sum of the non-constant map contribution to the topological free energy studied in
[
9
]
.

Figure 3:
On the left: plot of the rays
l_{k}=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}(t+k)
in the Borel-plane with
t=1/\pi(1+2\mathrm{i})
for
k\in(-250,250)
. On the right: plot of the rays
\widetilde{l}_{k}=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}(x+k)
(in blue) as well as
\widetilde{l}_{k,t}=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}(t+x+k)
(in orange) with
t=1/\pi(1+2\mathrm{i})
and
z=2+\mathrm{i}/3
for
k\in(-250,250)
.

Remark 5.3
.

•

Notice that the Stokes jumps (
5.2
) have the same form as the right-hand side of the difference equation (
4.14
) satisfied by
F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
. We will use this below to establish a relation between
W_{\rho}
and certain shifts of
F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)

•

In the previous results, we have restricted to the case
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
when discussing
W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
and the limits to
\pm l_{\infty}
. To do an analogous treatment for the limits to
\pm l_{\infty}
in the case
\operatorname{Im}(t)<0
, one would need to find an analogous relation of the form (
5.1
) that holds for
\operatorname{Im}(t)<0
, since the study of the limits to
\pm l_{\infty}
makes use of (
5.1
).

From the Stokes jumps of
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, together with (
5.1
) and the difference equation (
4.14
) satisfied by
F^{\rm NS}_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 5.4
.

Assume that
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
and
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
, and let
\rho_{k}
be a ray between
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Then

W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2+k),

and

W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)-F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(-\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2+k)

Proof.

We have from
(\ref{WBSR})
and our assumption on
t
that

W_{\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)=W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t)=F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2).

On the other hand, using the Stokes jumps of
W_{\rho}
and the difference equation (
4.14
), we find

W_{\rho_{1}}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}t/\check{\epsilon}}\big)=F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2+1)-F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2),

so that
W_{\rho_{1}}(\epsilon,t)=F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2+1)
. The result then follows from general
k\in\mathbb{Z}
by induction. Finally, the identity for
W_{-\rho_{k}}
follows from (
5.4
).
∎

On the other hand, by applying the same techniques used to compute the Borel sum and Stokes jumps of
W(\epsilon,t)
, we can compute the Borel sum and Stokes jumps of the formal series
S(\epsilon,x,t)
giving a formal solution to (
3.8
). More precisely, we have the following theorem

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
S(\epsilon,x,t)
be the formal series defined by

S(\epsilon,x,t)=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_{n}}{n!}(\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{n-1}({\rm Li}_{2-n}(QX)-{\rm Li}_{2-n}(X)),\qquad X=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}x),

giving a formal solution to

\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}(\epsilon,x,t)\Psi(\epsilon,x,t)=\big(\big(1-{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x}\big){\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}\partial_{x}}-\big(1-Q{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x}\big)\big)\Psi(\epsilon,x,t)=0

via

\Psi(\epsilon,x,t)=\exp(S(\epsilon,x,t)).

Then:

•

(Borel sum)
Let
t,x\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
x,t+x\notin\mathbb{Z}
and consider the rays in the
\epsilon
-plane defined by
\widetilde{l}_{k,t}:=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x+k)
and
\widetilde{l}_{k}:=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(x+k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
l_{\infty}:=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}

(
see Figure
3
on the right
)
. Then given any ray
\rho\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
from
0
to
\infty
different from
\big\{\pm\widetilde{l}_{k,t}\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\cup\big\{\pm\widetilde{l}_{k}\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{\pm l_{\infty}\}
; and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{\rho}
, where
\mathbb{H}_{\rho}
denotes the half-plane centered at
\rho
, the formal series
S(\epsilon,x,t)
is Borel summable with Borel sum:

\displaystyle S_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t):=-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\epsilon}(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(X))+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{Li}_{1}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(X))

\displaystyle\hphantom{S_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t) :=}{}+\int_{\rho}\mathrm{d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}G_{S}(\xi,x,t),

where

\displaystyle G_{S}(\xi,x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x)-\xi/m}-1}+\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x)+\xi/m}-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{G_S(\xi,x,t)=}{}-\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}x-\xi/m}-1}-\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}x+\xi/m}-1}\bigg).

We denote
\Psi_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t):=\exp(S_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t))
.

Furthermore, we have that for
\operatorname{Min}\{\operatorname{Im}(x),\operatorname{Im}(x+t)\}>0
,
\operatorname{Min}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}>0
,
\operatorname{Max}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<1
; the following holds for
\operatorname{Max}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
:

\Psi_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\exp(S_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,x,t))=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(t+x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x,t).

(5.5)

•

(Stokes jumps)
Assume that
t
and
x
are such that
\widetilde{l}_{k,t}
does not overlap
\pm\widetilde{l}_{r}
for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore assume that
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are two rays from
0
to
\infty
such that traversing
\rho^{\prime}
with the opposite orientation and then
\rho
gives a positively oriented Hankel contour. Finally, assume the sector determined by
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
contains only
\widetilde{l}_{k,t}
. Then

S_{\pm\rho}(\epsilon,x,t)-S_{\pm\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,x,t)=-\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(k+t+x)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

Similarly, if
\widetilde{l}_{k}
does not overlap with
\pm\widetilde{l}_{r,t}
for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are given as above but now the sector they define contains only
\widetilde{l}_{k}
, then

S_{\pm\rho}(\epsilon,x,t)-S_{\pm\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(k+x)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

In the case one rays overlaps another, the jump on the overlap is given by the sum of the jumps of the corresponding rays.

•

(Limits to
\pm l_{\infty}
)
Assume that
\text{min}\{\operatorname{Im}(x),\operatorname{Im}(x+t)\}>0
,
\text{min}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}>0
, and
\max\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<1
. Furthermore, let
\rho_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
be a sequence of rays, one for each of the sectors defined by
\widetilde{l}_{n}
and
\widetilde{l}_{m,t}
, and ordered in a clockwise manner. Then for
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)>0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)<0
and
\operatorname{Max}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\frac{L(x,\epsilon)}{L(x+t,\epsilon)}=:\Psi_{{\rm GV}}(x,\epsilon),

(5.6)

where
L(x,\epsilon)
is given in (
3.12
).
On the other hand, if
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)<0
and
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)<0
, while
\max\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<\operatorname{Re}(-\check{\epsilon}+1)
and
\max\big\{\big|{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}x/\check{\epsilon}}\big|,\big|{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(x+t)/\check{\epsilon}}\big|\big\}<1
then

\lim_{k\to-\infty}\Psi_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\Psi_{{\rm GV}}(x,\epsilon).

The limit to
-l_{\infty}
follows from the identities

\displaystyle S_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t)+S_{-\rho}(-\epsilon,x,t)=\mathrm{Li}_{1}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(X),

\displaystyle S(\epsilon,x,t)+S(-\epsilon,x,t)=\mathrm{Li}_{1}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(X).

In particular, this limit is also given by
\Psi_{{\rm GV}}
.

Similarly to
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, we can use (
5.5
) together with the difference equation (
B.4
) satisfied by
\mathcal{S}_{2}
to conclude the following:

Corollary 5.6
.

Assume that
\operatorname{Min}\{\operatorname{Im}(x),\operatorname{Im}(x+t)\}\!>0
,
\operatorname{Min}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}\!>0
,
\operatorname{Max}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\allowbreak\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<1
. Then if
\tilde{\rho}_{m,n}
is a ray between the rays
\widetilde{l}_{m,t}
and
\widetilde{l}_{m-1,t}
, and the rays
\widetilde{l}_{n}
and
\widetilde{l}_{n-1}
, then

\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{m,n}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+n|\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(t+x+m|\check{\epsilon},1)}=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x+n,t+m-n)

and

\Psi_{-{\tilde{\rho}_{m,n}}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\frac{1-X}{1-QX}\left(\Psi_{{\rm np}}(-\epsilon,x+n,t+m-n)\right)^{-1}.

Proof.

The proof for the first equality follows the same reasoning as Corollary
5.4
, but now using (
5.5
) together with the difference equation satisfied by
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, given in (
B.4
), and the Stokes jumps of
S_{\rho}
. For example, under our hypotheses, we have

\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{0,0}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\Psi_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x,t).

If the next ray to
\rho_{0,0}
in clockwise order is
\widetilde{l}_{0,t}
then

\displaystyle\frac{\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{1,0}}(\epsilon,x,t)}{\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{0,0}}(\epsilon,x,t)}

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(-\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\bigr)=1-{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x)/\check{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+t|\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+t+1|\check{\epsilon},1)}=\frac{\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x,t+1)}{\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x,t)},

so that

\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{1,0}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x,t+1).

If, on the other hand, the next ray to
\tilde{\rho}_{0,0}
in clockwise order is
\widetilde{l}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{0,1}}(\epsilon,x,t)}{\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{0,0}}(\epsilon,x,t)}=\exp\big(\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\big)=\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x/\check{\epsilon}}}=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+1|\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x|\check{\epsilon},1)}=\frac{\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x+1,t-1)}{\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x,t)},\textbf{}

so that

\Psi_{\tilde{\rho}_{0,1}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x+1,t-1).

The general identity then follows.

Last, the identity for
-\tilde{\rho}_{m,n}
follows from the first identity together with

S_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t)+S_{-\rho}(-\epsilon,x,t)=\mathrm{Li}_{1}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(X).

∎

Finally, we have the following theorem, relating
W_{\rho}
with
\Psi_{\rho}=\exp(S_{\rho})
.
To state the result, we consider the paths
X(s)={\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x(s)}
where
x(s)=\mathrm{i}s{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta
fixed. These paths connect the points
X=0
and
X=1
and will be important in Section
7
.

Theorem 5.7
.

Assume that
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
, while
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
, and let
\rho_{k}
be a ray between
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Furthermore, pick
x=\mathrm{i}s_{*}{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{*}}
such that
\operatorname{Im}(x)>0
, while
0<\operatorname{Re}(x)<1
, and
\rho_{k}
is of the form
\rho_{k,0}

(
recall the notation of Corollary
5.6
)
. Finally, consider the trajectory
x(s)=\mathrm{i}s{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{*}}
from
s=0
to
s=s_{*}
. Then, provided that
t+k\neq a\check{\epsilon}+b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we can analytically continue
\mathcal{S}_{2}(x(s)\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x(s),t)
along the trajectory
x(s)
from
x(s_{*})=\mathrm{i}s_{*}{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{*}}
to
x(0)=0
, and

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\big(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)\big)\big|_{x=0}.

In the following subsections, together with Appendix
A
, we give the proofs of Theorems
5.2
,
5.5
and
5.7
.

5.2
Borel transform

Before studying the Borel summability of
W(\epsilon,t)
, we must start by studying the Borel transform of the
\epsilon
-expansion of
W(\epsilon,t)
. Since
W(\epsilon,t)=F^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)
, we have by (
2.6
) that we can write the formal expansion

W(\epsilon,t)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{n}{\rm Li}_{3}(Q)\frac{B_{n}}{n!}\check{\epsilon}^{n-1}=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\rm Li}_{3}(Q)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}{\rm Li}_{2}(Q)+\frac{\epsilon}{12}{\rm Li}_{1}(Q)+\Phi(\check{\epsilon},t),

where (using that
B_{2n+1}=0
for
n>0
):

\Phi(\check{\epsilon},t):=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{n}{\rm Li}_{3}(Q)\frac{B_{n}}{n!}\check{\epsilon}^{n-1}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{2n}{\rm Li}_{3}(Q)\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!}\check{\epsilon}^{2n-1}.

We now wish to compute the Borel transform of
\Phi(\check{\epsilon},t)

and specify its domain of convergence.
The Borel transform is defined as the formal power series
G(\xi,t):=\mathcal{B}(\Phi(-,t))(\xi)
, where

\mathcal{B}\colon\ \check{\epsilon}\mathbb{C}[[\check{\epsilon}]]\to\mathbb{C}[[\xi]],\mathcal{B}(\check{\epsilon}^{n+1})=\frac{\xi^{n}}{n!}.

Namely, we wish to study

G(\xi,t)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!(2n-2)!}\xi^{2n-2}\partial_{t}^{2n}{\rm Li}_{3}(Q).

The main result about
G(\xi,t)
that we will prove and use is the following:

Proposition 5.8
.

Take
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
0<|\operatorname{Re}(t)|<1/2
and
|\epsilon/t|\in(0,2\pi)
, then
G(\xi,t)
converges. Furthermore, we have the alternate representation

G(\xi,t)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}-\{0\}}\frac{1}{m^{2}}\big(\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t+\xi/m}\big)+\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t-\xi/m}\big)-2\mathrm{Li}_{1}(Q)\big),

(5.7)

where
\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)=-\log(1-z)
uses the principal branch. In particular
G(\xi,t)
admits a continuation for all
\xi>0
.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is given in Appendix
A.1
. The techniques and ideas are the same as the ones used in
[
47
]
, which were also used in
[
9
]
to study the Borel transform of the topological free energy of the resolved conifold.
∎

On the other hand, the study of the Borel transform of
S(\epsilon,x,t)
follows from the same arguments in Appendix
A.1
used to study
G(\xi,t)
. One first writes

S(\epsilon,x,t)=-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\epsilon}(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(X))+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{Li}_{1}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(X))+\Phi_{S}(\check{\epsilon},x,t),

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{S}(\check{\epsilon},x,t):={}

\displaystyle{-}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!}(\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{2n-1}(\mathrm{Li}_{2-2n}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{2-2n}(X))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{-}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!}\check{\epsilon}^{2n-1}\partial_{x}^{2n}(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(X)).

We wish to study
G_{S}(\xi,x,t):=\mathcal{B}(\Phi_{S}(-,x,t))(\xi)
given by

G_{S}(\xi,x,t)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!}\frac{\xi^{2n-2}}{(2n-2)!}\partial_{x}^{2n}(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(X)).

(5.8)

By using the arguments of Appendix
A.1
with
G_{S}(\xi,x,t)=(f_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{0.1736pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\ast$}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 5.0pt}}f_{2}(-,x,t))(\xi)
, and

\displaystyle f_{1}(\xi):=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!}\xi^{2n-2},

\displaystyle f_{2}(\xi,x,t):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{2n-2}}{(2n-2)!}\partial_{x}^{2n}(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(QX)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(X)),

we find the following:

Proposition 5.9
.

Assume
t,x\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with
t\neq-x
and
\operatorname{Max}\{|\operatorname{Re}(x)|,|\operatorname{Re}(t+x)|\}<1/2
. Then for
|\xi|<\operatorname{Min}\{2\pi|t+x|,2\pi|x|\}
we have that
G(\xi,x,t)
in (
5.8
) converges. Furthermore, using the integral representation (
A.1
) for
G_{S}(\xi,x,t)=(f_{1}\mathbin{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\bigcirc$}$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.5pt\raisebox{0.1736pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\ast$}$}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip 5.0pt}}f_{2}(-,x,t))(\xi)
we find the alternate expression

\displaystyle G_{S}(\xi,x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m^{2}}\big(\mathrm{Li}_{0}\big(Q{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x+\xi/m}\big)+\mathrm{Li}_{0}\big(Q{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x-\xi/m}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{G_S(\xi,x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m\geq 1}}{}-\mathrm{Li}_{0}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x+\xi/m}\big)-\mathrm{Li}_{0}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x-\xi/m}\big)\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{G_S(\xi,x,t)}{}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x)-\xi/m}-1}+\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x)+\xi/m}-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{G_S(\xi,x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m\geq 1}}{}-\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}x-\xi/m}-1}-\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}x+\xi/m}-1}\bigg),

so that
G_{S}(\xi,x,t)
admits an analytic continuation in
\xi
to a meromorphic function with poles at
2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+x+k)m
and
2\pi\mathrm{i}(x+k)m
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}-\{0\}
.

5.3
Borel sum

We now study the Borel sum of the
\epsilon
-expansion of
W(\epsilon,t)
along
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. As before, we assume
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
0<|\operatorname{Re}(t)|<1/2
, so that
G(\xi,t)
admits a continuation for
\xi>0
with the expression (
5.7
), and we can consider the Borel sum along
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

In the following, we assume that
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
, where
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
denotes the half-plane centered at
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We then integrate by parts and use the fact that the boundary terms vanish to write

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}G(\xi,t)=\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathrm{d}\xi{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}-\{0\}}\frac{1}{m^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t+\xi/m}}-\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t-\xi/m}}\right).

The resulting expression in the integrand has poles at the points
\xi=2\pi{\rm i}(t+k)m
. We define

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}(\xi,t):={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}-\{0\}}\frac{1}{m^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t+\xi/m}}-\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t-\xi/m}}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{m\geq 1}\frac{1}{m^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t+\xi/m}}-\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t-\xi/m}}\right).

One advantage of the expression of the Borel sum with
\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)
is that we can now integrate freely along rays avoiding the poles, provided
\epsilon
is in the appropriate range. More precisely, we define:

Definition 5.10
.

For
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}-\mathbb{Z}
, for
\rho
a ray from
0
to
\infty
avoiding the rays
\pm l_{k}=\pm\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\pm l_{\infty}=\pm\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}
, and for
\check{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{H}_{\rho}
where
\mathbb{H}_{\rho}
denotes the half-plane centered at
\rho
, we denote the Borel sum of
W(\epsilon,t)
along
\rho
by

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t):=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{3}(Q)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)+\frac{\epsilon}{12}\mathrm{Li_{1}}(Q)+\check{\epsilon}\int_{\rho}\mathrm{d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t).

The arguments from Section
4.3
then motivate the consideration of the following function:

Definition 5.11
.

For
\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon})>0
and
-\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon})<\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
, we define

W_{\rm np}(\epsilon,t):=F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm ref}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2)=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)},

This function generates the quantum dilogarithm
\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
(see Appendix
B
or
[
21
]
), in the sense that

\partial_{t}W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)=2\pi\log\mathcal{S}_{2}(t|\check{\epsilon},1).

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=2\pi\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)}

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)}=2\pi\log\mathcal{S}_{2}(t\mid\check{\epsilon},1).

(5.9)

The function
W_{{\rm np}}
has the following relation to
W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
:

Proposition 5.12
.

If
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
and
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
, then for
\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
we have

W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t)=W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t).

Proof.

The proof of this is given in Appendix
A.2
. The proof follows the same lines of the corresponding proofs in
[
9
]
, showing the relation between the Borel sum along
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
of the non-constant map contribution of the topological free energy, and the triple sine function.
∎

On the other hand, we have the corresponding result for
S_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
:

Proposition 5.13
.

For
\operatorname{Min}\{\operatorname{Im}(x),\operatorname{Im}(x+t)\}>0
, as well as
\operatorname{Min}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}>0
, and
\operatorname{Max}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<1
, we have that

S_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1))-\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+t\mid\check{\epsilon},1)),

for
\operatorname{Max}\{\operatorname{Re}(x),\operatorname{Re}(x+t)\}<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
. Here,
\mathcal{S}_{2}
denotes the Faddeev quantum dilogarithm defined in Appendix
B
.

Proof.

The proof follows easier versions of the same argument given in Appendix
A.2
for
W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)
. More specifically, by following a similar computation to Proposition
A.4
, one shows that for
t,x\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Im}(t+x)>0
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

S_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\mathrm{d}s\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{s}}\big(\log\big(1-{\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s+2\pi\mathrm{i}x}\big)-\log\big(1-{\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s+2\pi\mathrm{i}(x+t)}\big)\big),

and then one shows that

\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x|\check{\epsilon},1))=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}+i0^{+}}\mathrm{d}s\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{s}}\log\big(1-{\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s+2\pi\mathrm{i}x}\big)

by following the argument of Proposition
A.5
, and by using the integral representation of
\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x|\check{\epsilon},1))
given in (
B.6
).
∎

5.4
Stokes jumps

In this section, we study the dependence of the Borel sum
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
on the choice of
\rho
. We start with the following result:

Proposition 5.14
.

Assume that
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
and for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
let
l_{k}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)
. Furthermore let
\rho
be a ray in the sector determined by the Stokes rays
l_{k+1}
and
l_{k}
, and
\rho^{\prime}
a ray in the sector determined by
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Then for
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{\rho}\cap\mathbb{H}_{\rho^{\prime}}

(
resp.
\epsilon\in\mathbb{H}_{-\rho}\cap\mathbb{H}_{-\rho^{\prime}})
we have

W_{\pm\rho}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\pm\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,t)=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

If
\operatorname{Im}(t)<0
, then the previous jumps also hold provided
\rho
is interchanged with
\rho^{\prime}
in the above formulas.

Proof.

Note that

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,t)=\check{\epsilon}\int_{\mathcal{H}(l_{k})}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t),

where
\mathcal{H}(l_{k})
is a Hankel contour around
l_{k}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)
.

Furthermore, note that we can write
\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)
as a sum over
m>0
as

\widetilde{G}(\xi,t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{m>0}\frac{1}{m^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t+\xi/m}}-\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}t-\xi/m}}\right).

We then have

\displaystyle\check{\epsilon}\int_{\mathcal{H}(l_{k})}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\big({\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}(\xi-2\pi\mathrm{i}m(t+k))\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)\big)\big|_{\xi=2\pi\mathrm{i}m(t+k)}

\displaystyle=-2\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{m=-1}^{-\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{-2\pi{\rm i}m(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}}{2\pi m^{2}}=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

A similar computation follows for
-l_{k}
.
∎

The computation for the Stokes jumps of
S_{\rho}(\epsilon,x,t)
follows exactly the argument as above.

5.5
Limits to
\boldsymbol{\pm l_{\infty}}

On the other hand, the jumps along
\pm l_{\infty}
will follow from the following proposition, which discusses the limits to
l_{\infty}
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\rho_{k}
denote any ray between the Stokes rays
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Furthermore, assume that
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
,
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)>0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)<0
, and
\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
. Then

\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t).

On the other hand, assume that
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
,
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)<0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)<0
,
\operatorname{Re}t<\operatorname{Re}(-\check{\epsilon}+1)
and that
\big|w^{-1}\big|<1
. Then

\begin{split}\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t).\end{split}

Proof.

This is proven in Appendix
A.3
, since the computation is rather lengthy. The main strategy for the first limit is to express it as a sum over the Stokes jumps in the corresponding quadrant and
W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
, and to compute
W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=W_{{\rm np}}
in terms of a sum over residues. For the second limit, we again sum over the Stokes jumps over the corresponding quadrant, and use the relation
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)+W_{-\rho}(-\epsilon,t)=-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)
to relate
W_{-\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
to
W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
and hence express it as a sum over residues.
∎

To compute the other limits, notice that we have the easy to check relations

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)+W_{-\rho}(-\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q),

(5.10)

and

W(\epsilon,t)+W(-\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q).

(5.11)

From this, the next corollary follows, discussing the limits to
-l_{\infty}
:

Corollary 5.16
.

For
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
and
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
, whereas
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)<0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)>0
and
\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(-\check{\epsilon}+1)
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t)-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q).

On the other hand, assume that
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
,
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Re}(\epsilon)>0
,
\operatorname{Im}(\epsilon)>0
and
\operatorname{Re}(t)<\operatorname{Re}(\check{\epsilon}+1)
while
\big|{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t/\check{\epsilon}}\big|<1
. Then

\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q).

Proof.

By using Proposition
5.15
and the identities (
5.10
) and (
5.11
), we get

\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=-\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)=-W(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)=W(\epsilon,t).

Similarly,

\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=-\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)=-W(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)=W(\epsilon,t).

∎

Finally, using the same idea as in the proof of Proposition
5.15
, one can prove the limit (
5.6
) for
\Psi_{\rho}
. In fact, due to the relation

\Psi_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,x,t)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+t\mid\check{\epsilon},1)},

it is enough to use the integral representation of
\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x|\check{\epsilon},1))
and the jumps across
\widetilde{l}_{k}
, and show that

\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1))+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}(k+x)/\check{\epsilon}))=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}lx}}{l({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}l\check{\epsilon}}-1)}=\log(L(x,\epsilon)),

(5.12)

since then the jumps along
\widetilde{l}_{k,t}
combine with
-\log(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+t\mid\check{\epsilon},1))
to give
-\log(L(x+t,\epsilon))
.

5.6
A potential for the Borel sums

The main result we wish to prove in the section is the following, showing that
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
serves as a potential for the Borel sums of the non-constant map contribution of the topological free energy:

Proposition 5.17
.

The
\epsilon
-derivative of the Stokes jumps of
W_{\rho}
equal the Stokes jumps of
F_{\rho}
. Furthermore, we have

\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)=F_{\rho}(\epsilon,t),

(5.13)

where
F_{\rho}
denotes the Borel sum of the non-constant map contribution to the topological free energy studied in
[
9
]
.

Proof.

The first statement on the
\epsilon
-derivative is clear. Indeed, we have

\partial_{\epsilon}\bigl(-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\bigr)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\check{\epsilon}}\big(\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\big),

matching the jumps of
F_{\rho}
of
[
9
]
under the identification
\epsilon=\lambda
, where
\lambda
denotes the topological string coupling.

Now recall that by Proposition
5.12
, we have that on their common domains of definition

W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t)=W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t).

In particular, we obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\epsilon}W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)=\partial_{\epsilon}\left(2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s}\frac{{\rm e}^{s(t+\check{\epsilon})}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)^{2}}=F_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t).

where in the last equality we have used
[
9
, Theorem 2]
.

Now let
l_{k}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)
as before, and let
\rho_{k}
be a ray between
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. The fact that

\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=F_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t).

follows from the fact that
\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\rho_{0}}=\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=F_{{\rm np}}=F_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
, together with the fact that the
\epsilon
-derivatives of the Stokes jumps of
W_{\rho}
equal those of
F_{\rho}
.

On the other hand, to check this for
-\rho_{k}
, we use the following easily verifiable relations

W_{-\rho}(\epsilon,t)=-W_{\rho}(-\epsilon,t)+i\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q),\qquad F_{-\rho}(\epsilon,t)=F_{\rho}(-\epsilon,t).

It then follows that

\partial_{\epsilon}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=-\partial_{\epsilon}(W_{\rho_{k}}(-\epsilon,t)-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q))=F_{\rho_{k}}(-\epsilon,t)=F_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t).

The result then follows.
∎

Remark 5.18
.

Analogously, one can prove (
5.13
) by computing the
\epsilon
-derivative of the Borel sum expression of
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, and show that it matches (after an integration by parts in one of the summands) the expression of the Borel sum
F_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
of
[
9
]
. The statement of the jumps then follows automatically.

5.7
Relation between
\boldsymbol{\Psi_{\rho}}
and
\boldsymbol{W_{\rho}}

In this subsection we prove Theorem
5.7
:

Theorem
5.7
.

Assume that
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
, while
0<\operatorname{Re}(t)<1
, and let
\rho_{k}
be a ray between
l_{k}
and
l_{k-1}
. Furthermore, pick
x=\mathrm{i}s_{*}{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{*}}
such that
\operatorname{Im}(x)>0
, while
0<\operatorname{Re}(x)<1
, and
\rho_{k}
is of the form
\tilde{\rho}_{k,0}

(
recall the notation of Corollary
5.6
)
.
12
12

12

For example, we can pick
0<s_{*}
with
s_{*}
sufficiently close to
0
and
-\pi/2<\theta_{*}<0
with
\theta_{*}
sufficiently close to
-\pi/2
.

Finally, consider the trajectory
x(s)=\mathrm{i}s{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{*}}
from
s=0
to
s=s_{*}
. Then, provided that
t+k\neq a\check{\epsilon}+b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we can analytically continue
\mathcal{S}_{2}(x(s)\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x(s),t)
along the trajectory
x(s)
from
x(s_{*})=\mathrm{i}s_{*}{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{*}}
to
x(0)=0
, and

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\big(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)\big)\big|_{x=0}.

(5.14)

Proof.

Recall that by Corollary
5.4
and equation (
5.9
),

\partial_{t}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=\partial_{t}F_{{\rm np}}^{\rm NS}(\epsilon,t-\check{\epsilon}/2+k)=2\pi\log\mathcal{S}_{2}(t+k\mid\check{\epsilon},1).

On the other hand, note that for all
s
such that
0<s\leq s_{*}
we continue to satisfy that
\rho_{k}=\tilde{\rho}_{k,0}
. We can therefore apply Corollary
5.6
and write

\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x(s),t)=\Psi_{\rho_{k,0}}(\epsilon,x(s),t)=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x(s),t+k)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x(s)\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x(s)+t+k\mid\check{\epsilon},1)},

so that by taking the limit to
s\to 0
we obtain

\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)|_{x=0}=\mathcal{S}_{2}(t+k\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}.

The previous quantity is well defined due to the contraint
t+k\neq a\check{\epsilon}+b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
(recall point
1
of Appendix
B.2
).
The desired equation (
5.14
) then follows.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

It may be helpful to note that the rays
\tilde{l}_{k,t}
approximate the rays
l_{k}
when
x\to 0
, while the rays
\tilde{l}_{m}
for
m<0
(or
m>0
) collapse into the positive (or negative) imaginary axis when
x\to 0
, and the phase of the ray
\tilde{l}_{0}
is equal to
\vartheta_{*}
along the trajectory
x(s)
. It is thus impossible to choose the fixed ray
\rho_{k}
of the form
\tilde{\rho}_{k,m}
for
m\neq\{0,1\}
when
x\to 0
.

With the restrictions on
x
as in the assumptions of the above theorem, the phase
\vartheta_{*}
along the trajectory
x(s)
should be in between 0 and
-\pi/2
. This means that we can only choose
\rho_{k}
of the form
\tilde{\rho}_{k,1}
when the phase of
\rho_{k}
is in between 0 and
-\pi/2
(in which case
\vartheta_{*}
should be chosen in between 0 and the phase of
\rho_{k}
, and equation (
5.14
) would need to be modified with a small factor). On the other hand, if we choose
\rho_{k}
to be of the form
\tilde{\rho}_{k,0}
, this means
\vartheta_{*}
should be chosen smaller than 0 while in between the phase of
\rho_{k}
and
-\pi/2
. This can be achieved by choosing
\vartheta_{*}
sufficiently close to
-\pi/2
as in footnote (
12
).

We remark that the restrictions on
x
in the above theorem are mainly there to be able to apply Corollary
5.4
, which in turn uses these restrictions to be able to say that
\Psi_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\Psi_{{\rm np}}
. The restrictions on
x
in the above theorem are however not stringent and can be relaxed at the cost of modifying the statement (
5.14
) slightly.

Corollary 5.20
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
5.7
and the assumptions on
\epsilon
from Proposition
5.15
, we can take the limit
k\to\infty
and obtain

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}W_{\mathrm{GV}}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\big(L(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\mathrm{GV}}(\epsilon,x,t)\big)\big|_{x=0}.

Here we have introduced the notation
W_{{\rm GV}}(\epsilon,t):=W(\epsilon,t)
.

Proof.

Using our previous results, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)|_{x=0}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{S}_{2}(t+k\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}

\displaystyle=L(t,\check{\epsilon})^{-1}=\big(L(x,\check{\epsilon})^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\mathrm{GV}}(\epsilon,x,t)\big)\big|_{x=0}.

In the computation of the limit above, we have used equation (
5.12
) together with

\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x+1\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}=\frac{1}{1-{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}x/\check{\epsilon}}}.

The result then follows from Proposition
5.15
.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

In the setting of the previous theorem, one has

\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x(s),t)=\Psi_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,x(s),t+k)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x(s)\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(x(s)+t+k\mid\check{\epsilon},1)}

for
0<s\leq s_{*}
, since
\rho_{k}
is of the form
\rho_{k,0}
.
We can use the right-hand side of the above equation to analytically continue
\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)
by taking
s\to\infty
. If we further assume that
\operatorname{Im}(\check{\epsilon})>0
, we can use the product formula of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
given in (
B.5
) to conclude that

\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x(s),t)|_{s=\infty}=1.

In Section
7
, we will consider exponentiated trajectories of the form
X(s)={\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}x(s)}={\rm e}^{-2\pi s{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta_{*}}}
connecting
X=0
and
X=1
. The previous result motivates the definition of the following “regularized” period:

Definition 5.22
.

In the setting of Theorem
5.7
and Corollary
5.20
, we define the regularized quantum period

\Pi_{B,\rho_{k}}^{\rm reg}:=\log\frac{\big(\mathcal{S}_{2}(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)\big)\big|_{x=0}}{\Psi_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,x,t)|_{x=\mathrm{i}\infty}},

and its
k\to\infty
limit

\Pi_{B,\mathrm{GV}}^{\rm reg}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Pi_{B,\rho_{k}}^{\rm reg}=\log\frac{\big(L(x\mid\check{\epsilon},1)^{-1}\cdot\Psi_{\mathrm{GV}}(\epsilon,x,t)\big)\big|_{x=0}}{\Psi_{\mathrm{GV}}(\epsilon,x,t)|_{x=\mathrm{i}\infty}}.

One can then interpret equation (
5.14
) as a relation between the NS free energies
W
and the corresponding regularized exact quantum
B
-periods
\Pi_{B}^{\rm reg}
. Namely,

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\exp\big(\Pi_{B,\rho_{k}}^{\rm reg}\big),

and its
k\to\infty
limit

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}W_{\mathrm{GV}}(\epsilon,t)\right)=\exp\big(\Pi_{B,\mathrm{GV}}^{\rm reg}\big),

with again
W_{{\rm GV}}(\epsilon,t):=W(\epsilon,t)
.
This perspective will be useful later in Section
7
.

6
DT invariants and line bundles

In
[
9
]
, the Borel summability of the Gromov–Witten (GW) potential for the resolved conifold is studied, giving the following result for the Borel sum along a ray
\rho

[
9
, equation (4.35)]
:

\widehat{F}_{\rho}(\lambda,t)=F_{\rho}(\lambda,t)-F_{\rho}(\lambda,0)-\frac{\log(\check{\lambda})}{12}.

(6.1)

Here
t
denotes the Kähler parameter as before, and
\lambda
(
\check{\lambda}=\lambda/2\pi
) denotes the topological string coupling.
F_{\rho}(\lambda,t)
denotes the Borel sum of the non-constant map contribution of the GW potential, while
-F_{\rho}(\lambda,0)-\log\big(\check{\lambda}\big)/12
is shown to be (up to the addition of a constant) the Borel sum of constant map contribution of the GW potential.
F_{\rho}(\lambda,0)
is defined in terms of
F_{\rho}(\lambda,t)
by a certain limit in
t\to 0
(see
[
9
, Section 4.2.1]
for more details).

The Borel sum
\hat{F}_{\rho}
experiences Stokes jumps in the
\lambda
-plane along the rays
\pm l_{k}
and
\pm l_{\infty}
from before, with the jumps containing the information of the DT invariants of the resolved conifold in the following way (we assume below that
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
):

•

Let
M
be the space parametrized by the
t
-parameter and consider
\Gamma\to M
a trivial rank-
2
local system of lattices spanned by
\beta
and
\delta
. The BPS indices of the resolved conifold are then expressed as

\Omega(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1&\quad\text{if }\gamma=\pm\beta+n\delta\quad\text{for }n\in\mathbb{Z},\\
-2&\quad\text{if }\gamma=k\delta\quad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},\\
\hphantom{-}0&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We further consider the central charge function
Z
(a holomorphic section of
{\Gamma^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}\to M}
) defined by

Z_{n\beta+m\delta}=2\pi\mathrm{i}(nt+m),\qquad n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

•

Along
\pm l_{k}=\pm\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)=\pm\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot Z_{\beta+k\delta}
the jump of
\hat{F}_{\rho}
encodes
\Omega(\beta+k\delta)
as

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\check{\lambda}}\big(\check{\lambda}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\lambda}}\big)\big)=\frac{\Omega(\beta+k\delta)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\check{\lambda}}\big(\check{\lambda}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm Z_{\beta+k\delta}/\check{\lambda}}\big)\big).

(6.2)

•

Along
\pm l_{\infty}=\pm\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot Z_{\pm k\delta}
the jumps of
\hat{F}_{\rho}
encode the
\Omega(k\delta)
via

\sum_{k\geq 1}-\frac{1}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\check{\lambda}}\big(\check{\lambda}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\lambda}}\big)\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\Omega(k\delta)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\check{\lambda}}\big(\check{\lambda}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm Z_{k\delta}/\check{\lambda}}\big)\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}.

(6.3)

We remark that the interpretation of the coefficients in front of the
\mathrm{Li}_{2}
summands as the DT invariants comes from the relation done in
[
9
, Section 4.2]
to the Riemann–Hilbert (RH) problem associated to the resolved conifold
[
21
]
. It is shown that the jumps of
\hat{F}_{\rho}
serve as potentials for the jumps of the RH problem, involving the DT invariants
[
9
, Corollary 4.13]
.

We would like to show that a properly normalized
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
also encodes the DT invariants of the resolved conifold, in a similar way. Given the relation

\partial_{\epsilon}W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)=F_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)

shown in (
5.13
), together with (
6.1
),
a natural object to consider is

\widehat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t):=W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)-\frac{\epsilon}{12}\log(\check{\epsilon})+\frac{\pi}{6}\epsilon,

(6.4)

where
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)
is defined by a certain limit
t\to 0
. We discuss this limit and the Stokes jumps of
\hat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
below. We then establish the link between the jumps of
\hat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
and the DT invariant of the resolved conifold.

On the other hand, in
[
9
, Section 4]
, a certain projectivized version of
\hat{F}_{\rho}
is shown to define a section of a line bundle, related to a conformal limit of a hyperholomorphic line bundle discussed in
[
5
,
84
]
. We show below that (a projectived version of)
\hat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
can be thought as defining a section of the same line bundle as the one associated to
\hat{F}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
.

6.1
Normalized
\boldsymbol{W_{\rho}}
and the DT invariants

In this section, we wish to show how the DT invariants of the resolved conifold as encoded in the Stokes jumps of a properly normalized
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
. To show how the
-2
BPS index is contained in the Borel sums
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, we will first study a limit of the form

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0):=\lim_{t\to 0}W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t),

where
t
is taken to satisfy
\operatorname{Re}(t)>0
,
\operatorname{Im}(t)>0
; and such that along the limit,
\rho
is always between
l_{-1}
and
l_{0}
(resp.
-l_{-1}
and
-l_{0}
) if
\rho
is on the right (resp. left) Borel half-plane. This is analogous to the way
F_{\rho}(\lambda,0)
was defined in
[
9
]
.

To see that this gives a well defined limit, notice that when
\rho
is on the right Borel plane we can write

\displaystyle W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{3}(Q)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)+\frac{\epsilon}{12}\mathrm{Li_{1}}(Q)+\check{\epsilon}\int_{\rho}\mathrm{d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{3}(Q)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)+\check{\epsilon}\int_{\rho}\mathrm{d}\xi\left({\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)+\frac{\pi}{6}\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{\xi-2\pi{\rm i}t}-1}\right).

Now when
t\to 0
as above, we see that
{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,0)
develops a simple pole at
\xi=0
of the from
-\frac{\pi}{6\xi}
, which gets canceled with the simple pole arising from the second term. Furthermore, no Stokes rays
l_{k}
cross
\rho
in the above limit in
t
, so we get a well defined limit. When
\rho
is on the left Borel plane, we can reduce to the previous case by using the identity
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)=-W_{-\rho}(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)
. Hence, we have a well defined limit

W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{3}(1)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1)+\lim_{t\to 0}\left(\check{\epsilon}\int_{\rho}\mathrm{d}\xi\left({\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)+\frac{\pi}{6}\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{\xi-2\pi\mathrm{i}t}-1}\right)\right),\\

where
t
satisfies the constraints specified above.

The following proposition suggests that one can obtain the appropriate Stokes jumps at
\pm l_{\infty}
by considering a normalization of
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
involving
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)
:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\rho

(
resp.
\rho^{\prime})
be a ray close to
l_{\infty}=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}
from the left
(
resp. right
)
. Then for
\epsilon
in their common domain of definition

W_{\pm\rho}(\epsilon,0)-W_{\pm\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,0)=-2\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\epsilon}}\big)-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\epsilon.

Furthermore
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)
only has Stokes jumps along
\pm l_{\infty}
.

Proof.

First, notice that by our definition of the limit in
t
, we have

\begin{split}W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)-W_{\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,0)&=\lim_{t\to 0}\bigg(\int_{\rho}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)-\int_{\rho^{\prime}}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)\bigg)\\
&=\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{H}}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{H}=\rho-\rho^{\prime}
denotes a Hankel contour along
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}
, containing
l_{k}
for
k\geq 0
and
-l_{k}
for
k<0
. Hence, for
\epsilon
close to
l_{\infty}
, the Hankel contour just gives the contribution of these rays that we previously computed:

\displaystyle W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)-W_{\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon,0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{H}}{\rm d}\xi\,{\rm e}^{-\xi/\check{\epsilon}}\widetilde{G}(\xi,t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\biggl(-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k\geq 1}\big[\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)+\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(t-k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\big]-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t/\check{\epsilon}})\biggr)

\displaystyle=-2\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\epsilon}}\big)-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1)=-2\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\epsilon}}\big)-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\epsilon.

A similar argument follows for
-l_{\infty}=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Furthermore, the fact that there are no other Stokes jumps follows from the way we have defined the limit
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)
.
∎

By the previous arguments, we would like to now consider:

\widehat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)=W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\rho}(\epsilon,0)-\frac{\epsilon}{12}\log(\check{\epsilon})+\frac{\pi}{6}\epsilon,

where the
\log
-term has a branch cut at
l_{\infty}
, and study its Stokes jumps.

Corollary 6.2
.

The Stokes jumps of
\widehat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
along
\pm l_{k}
are given by the same jumps as
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, while the Stokes jumps at
\pm l_{\infty}
are given by

2\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\epsilon.

In particular, the
\epsilon
-derivatives of the Stokes jumps of
\hat{W}_{\rho}
give the Stokes jumps of
\hat{F}_{\rho}
.

Joining the results of the previous corollary, together with the way the DT invariants are encoded in terms of the Stokes jumps of
\hat{F}_{\rho}
, given in (
6.2
) and (
6.3
), we find that:

Corollary 6.3
.

The DT invariants of the resolved conifold are encoded in the Stokes jumps of
\hat{W}_{\rho}
in the following way:

•

Along
\pm l_{k}=\pm\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)=\pm\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot Z_{\beta+k\delta}
the jump of
\hat{W}_{\rho}
encodes
\Omega(\beta+k\delta)
as

-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)=\frac{\Omega(\beta+k\delta)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\epsilon\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm Z_{\beta+k\delta}/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

•

Along
\pm l_{\infty}=\pm\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}_{<0}=\mathbb{R}_{<0}\cdot Z_{\pm k\delta}
the jumps of
\hat{W}_{\rho}
encode the
\Omega(k\delta)
via

2\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}k/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\epsilon=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\Omega(k\delta)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\epsilon\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm Z_{k\delta}/\check{\epsilon}}\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\epsilon.

6.2
Line bundle defined by
\boldsymbol{\hat{W}_{\rho}}

In
[
9
]
, it turned out to be convenient to projectize the Kähler parameter
t
and the topological string coupling
\lambda
as

\lambda=2\pi\lambda_{B}/w,\qquad t=v/w,

in order to relate to the Riemann–Hilbert problem considered in
[
20
,
21
]
. Furthermore, the projectivized partition function

Z^{{\rm top}}_{\rho}(v,w,\lambda_{B}):=\exp\big(\widehat{F}_{w^{-1}\cdot\rho}(2\pi\lambda_{B}/w,v/w)\big)

was considered, which reduces to the usual partition function when
w=1
. That is,

Z^{{\rm top}}_{\rho}(v,1,\lambda_{B}):=\exp\big(\widehat{F}_{\rho}(2\pi\lambda_{B},v)\big)=\exp\big(\widehat{F}_{\rho}(\lambda,t)\big).

The projectivized free energy
\widehat{F}_{w^{-1}\cdot\rho}(2\pi\lambda_{B}/w,v/w)
now has Stokes jumps in the
\lambda_{B}
variable along
\pm\mathcal{L}_{k}:=\pm w\cdot l_{k}
and
\pm\mathcal{L}_{\infty}:=\pm w\cdot l_{\infty}
. Provided
\operatorname{Im}(v/w)>0
, these are given by

•

Along
\pm\mathcal{L}_{k}
the jump is

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\lambda_{B}}\big(\lambda_{B}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(v+kw)/\lambda_{B}}\big)\big).

•

Along
\pm\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
the jump is

\sum_{k\geq 1}-\frac{1}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\lambda_{B}}\big(\lambda_{B}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi{\rm i}kw/\lambda_{B}}\big)\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}.

The exponentials of such jumps were then interpreted as specifying the transition functions of a line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm top}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times M_{+}
having a global section specified by the Borel sums
Z_{\rho}^{{\rm top}}(v,w,\lambda_{B})
, and where

M_{+}:=\{(v,w)\mid\operatorname{Im}(v/w)>0\}.

The line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm top}}
was then shown to correspond to a certain conformal limit of hyperholomorphic line bundles previously considered in
[
5
,
84
]
.

In this section we wish to consider an analogous projectivized version of
\hat{W}_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
, and show that it defines a section of the same line bundle from before.

We start by recalling more specifically how
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm top}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times M_{+}
is defined. Let
\rho_{k}
be the middle ray between
\mathcal{L}_{k}
and
\mathcal{L}_{k-1}
, and consider the open subsets

U_{k}^{\pm}:=\{(\lambda_{B},v,w)\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times M_{+}\mid\lambda_{B}\in\mathbb{H}_{\pm\rho_{k}}\}.

Then
\{U_{k}^{+}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{U_{k}^{-}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
form an open covering of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times M_{+}
and
Z^{{\rm top}}_{\pm\rho_{k}}(\lambda_{B},v,w)
is defined on
U_{k}^{\pm}
. We then define a 1-Čech cocycle specifying
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm top}}
and associated to the previous cover as follows:

•

If
U_{k_{1}}^{\pm}\cap U_{k_{2}}^{\pm}\neq\varnothing
for
k_{1}<k_{2}
, we then define for
(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w)\in U_{k_{1}}^{\pm}\cap U_{k_{2}}^{\pm}
,

g^{\pm}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w):=\prod_{k_{1}\leq k<k_{2}}\Xi_{\pm\mathcal{L}_{k}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w),

where

\Xi_{\pm\mathcal{L}_{k}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\lambda_{B}}\big(\lambda_{B}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(v+kw)/\lambda_{B}}\big)\big)\right).

•

On the other hand, if for some
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
U_{k_{1}}^{+}\cap U_{k_{2}}^{-}\neq\varnothing
, then
\rho_{k_{1}}\neq\rho_{k_{2}}
and hence out of the two sectors determined by
\rho_{k_{1}}
and
-\rho_{k_{2}}
there is a smallest one, which we denote by
[\rho_{k_{1}},-\rho_{k_{2}}]
. For all
(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w)\in U_{k_{1}}^{+}\cap U_{k_{2}}^{-}
we must either have that
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}\subset[\rho_{k_{1}},-\rho_{k_{2}}]
or
-\mathcal{L}_{\infty}\subset[\rho_{k_{1}},-\rho_{k_{2}}]
. In the first case we define

g_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\infty}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w):={\rm e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}/12}\prod_{k\geq k_{1}}\Xi_{\mathcal{L}_{k}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w)\prod_{k<k_{2}}\Xi_{-\mathcal{L}_{k}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\Xi_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty},k}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w),

and
g_{k_{2},k_{1}}^{\infty}:=\big(g_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{\infty}\big)^{-1}
, where

\Xi_{\pm\mathcal{L}_{\infty},k}(v,w,\lambda_{\mathrm{B}})=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\partial_{\lambda_{\mathrm{B}}}\big(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm kw/\lambda_{\mathrm{B}}}\big)\big)\right);

while in the second case we define

g_{k_{2},k_{1}}^{-\infty}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w):={\rm e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}/12}\prod_{k\geq k_{2}}\Xi_{-\mathcal{L}_{k}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w)\prod_{k<k_{1}}\Xi_{\mathcal{L}_{k}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w)\prod_{k\geq 1}\Xi_{-\mathcal{L}_{\infty},k}(\lambda_{\mathrm{B}},v,w),

and
g_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{-\infty}:=\big(g_{k_{2},k_{1}}^{-\infty}\big)^{-1}
.

We now define a projectived version of
W_{\rho}(\epsilon,t)
. As before, we consider the projectivized variables

\epsilon=2\pi\epsilon_{B}/w,\qquad t=v/w,

and

Z_{\rho}^{\rm NS}(v,w,\epsilon_{B}):=\exp\big(\widehat{W}_{w^{-1}\cdot\rho}(2\pi\epsilon/w,v/w)\big).

From the same analysis as before, we obtain that the jumps of
\widehat{W}_{w^{-1}\cdot\rho}(2\pi\epsilon/w,v/w)
along
\pm\mathcal{L}_{k}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
are given by

•

Along
\pm\mathcal{L}_{k}
the jump is

-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(v+kw)/\epsilon_{B}}\big).

•

Along
\pm\mathcal{L}_{\infty}
the jump is

\sum_{k\geq 1}2\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi{\rm i}kw/\epsilon_{B}}\big)\mp\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{12}\frac{2\pi\epsilon_{B}}{w}.

Under the identification
\epsilon_{B}=\lambda_{B}
, we have that
Z_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}
is defined on
U_{k}^{\pm}
, and by using the exponentials of the Stokes jumps of
Z_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}
we can as before construct a line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{\rm NS}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times M_{+}
such that
Z_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}
define a section of
\mathcal{L}^{\rm NS}
.

To show that
\mathcal{L}^{\rm NS}=\mathcal{L}^{{\rm top}}
it is enough to show that we can scale each section
Z_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}
over
U_{k}^{\pm}
by a function defined on
U_{k}^{\pm}
such that we recover the same transition functions of
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm top}}
.

For this we will use the functions
f_{\pm\rho_{k}}(\epsilon_{B},v,w)\colon U_{k}^{\pm}\to\mathbb{C}
defined in
[
9
, Lemma 4.18]
, which satisfy

f_{\pm\rho_{k+1}}(\epsilon_{B},v,w)-f_{\pm\rho_{k}}(\epsilon_{B},v,w)=\pm\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}(v+kw)}{\epsilon_{B}}\log\big(1-{\rm e}^{\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i}(v+kw)/\epsilon_{B}}\big).

We then scale

Z_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}\to\widetilde{Z}_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}:=s_{\pm\rho_{k}}Z_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS},

where
s_{\pm\rho_{k}}\colon U_{k}^{\pm}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is defined by

s_{\pm\rho_{k}}(\epsilon_{B},v,w):=\exp\big((1-\epsilon)\cdot\widehat{F}_{\pm w^{-1}\cdot\rho_{k}}(2\pi\lambda_{B}/w,v/w)\big)\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}f_{\pm\rho_{k}}(\epsilon_{B},v,w)\right),

and where we recall that
\epsilon=2\pi\epsilon_{B}/w
.

One then finds that

\displaystyle\log\big(\widetilde{Z}_{\pm\rho_{k+1}}^{\rm NS}\big)-\log\big(\widetilde{Z}_{\pm\rho_{k}}^{\rm NS}\big)
