Ff_{\delta}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}y\,{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}\mathrm{Li}_{2}({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}y+2\pi\mathrm{i}t})=\frac{\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm d}y\,{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}\mathrm{Li}_{1}\big({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}y+2\pi\mathrm{i}t}\big)=\left(\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm d}y\,\frac{{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}}{1-{\rm e}^{-\check{\epsilon}y-2\pi\mathrm{i}t}},

where we have integrated by parts, and used that the boundary terms vanish. The last integral has simple poles at
y=2\pi\mathrm{i}(k-t)/\check{\epsilon}
, and under our assumptions for the parameters
t
and
\epsilon
, it is easy to check that the poles on the upper half-plane correspond to
k>0
, while those in the lower half-plane correspond to
k\leq 0
. If
\operatorname{Re}(x)>0
, by an application of Jordan’s lemma and the residue theorem, we can compute
Ff_{\delta}(x)
by summing up the residues in the upper half-plane, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm d}y\frac{{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}}{1-{\rm e}^{-\check{\epsilon}y-2\pi\mathrm{i}t}}

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta}\right)^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}}{\check{\epsilon}}\Big|_{y=2\pi\mathrm{i}(k-t)/\check{\epsilon}}=\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}{\rm e}^{2\pi t\zeta/\check{\epsilon}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{-2\pi k\zeta/\check{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}{\rm e}^{\pi\zeta(2t-1)/\check{\epsilon}}\left(2{\rm e}^{-\pi\zeta/\check{\epsilon}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{-2\pi\zeta k/\check{\epsilon}}\right)=\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{\pi\zeta(2t-1)/\check{\epsilon}}}{\sinh(\pi\zeta/\check{\epsilon})},

where in the last equality we have used the Dirichlet series representation of
1/\sinh(z)
. Similarly, if
\operatorname{Re}(x)<0
, we can compute
Ff_{\epsilon}
summing up the residues in the lower half-plane, obtaining

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\rm d}y\frac{{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}}{1-{\rm e}^{-\check{\epsilon}y-2\pi\mathrm{i}t}}

\displaystyle=-2\pi\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta}\right)^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{-\infty}\frac{{\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}y\zeta}}{\check{\epsilon}}\Big|_{y=2\pi\mathrm{i}(k-t)/\check{\epsilon}}=-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}{\rm e}^{2\pi t\zeta/\check{\epsilon}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{2\pi k\zeta/\check{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}{\rm e}^{\pi\zeta(2t-1)/\check{\epsilon}}\left(-2{\rm e}^{\pi\zeta/\check{\epsilon}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{2\pi\zeta k/\check{\epsilon}}\right)=\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{\pi\zeta(2t-1)/\check{\epsilon}}}{\sinh(\pi\zeta/\check{\epsilon})},

so that
Ff_{\delta}(x)
exists for
x\neq 0
and

Ff_{\delta}(x)=\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{\zeta^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{\pi\zeta(2t-1)/\check{\epsilon}}}{\sinh(\pi\zeta/\check{\epsilon})}.

Similarly, we can compute
Fg_{\delta}(x)
, obtaining that for
x\neq 0
,

\overline{Fg_{\delta}(x)}=\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{{\rm e}^{(\delta-\pi)\zeta}}{\sinh(\pi\zeta)}.

We then have that

\begin{split}\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\langle f_{\delta},g_{\delta}\rangle&=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\langle Ff_{\delta},Fg_{\delta}\rangle\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}{\rm d}x\left(\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{(2\pi x)^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{\pi(2\pi x)(2t-1)/\check{\epsilon}}}{\sinh(2\pi^{2}x/\check{\epsilon})}\right)\left(\frac{\pi{\rm i}{\rm e}^{-2\pi^{2}x}}{\sinh(2\pi^{2}x)}\right)\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}+{\rm i}0^{+}}{\rm d}x\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}}{(2\pi x)^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{4\pi^{2}xt/\check{\epsilon}}}{{\rm e}^{4\pi^{2}x/\check{\epsilon}}-1}\right)\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{{\rm e}^{4\pi^{2}x}-1}\right)\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\check{\epsilon}^{-1}\cdot(\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+})}\frac{{\rm d}s}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{(2\pi\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon})^{2}}{(s\check{\epsilon}/2\pi)^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)},\qquad s=(2\pi)^{2}x/\check{\epsilon}\\
&=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)},\end{split}

where we used the fact that the range of the parameter
\epsilon
allows us to deform the contour back to
\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}
. The result then follows. In particular, from Proposition
A.4
it follows that

W_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}(\epsilon,t)=W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)

on their common domains of definition.
∎

A.3
Proof of the limits to
\boldsymbol{l_{\infty}}

Here we prove Proposition
5.15
, dealing with the sided limits to
l_{\infty}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.14
, we find that

W_{\rho_{k}}-W_{\rho_{k+1}}=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big).

Denoting
w={\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t/\check{\epsilon}}
and
\widetilde{q}={\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/\check{\epsilon}}
, we find

\displaystyle W_{\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)-\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\rho_{k+1}}(\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w\widetilde{q}^{k}\big).

We now use the following identity:

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w\widetilde{q}^{k}\big)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l^{2}}\frac{w^{l}}{1-\widetilde{q}^{l}}.

(A.5)

In order to verify (
A.5
), we use that
|\widetilde{q}|<1
and
|w|<1
to see that both sides converge. One can then act on both sides with
w\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}w}
.
The left side of the resulting equation is easily seen to be equal to

-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\log\big(1-w\widetilde{q}^{k}\big)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l}\frac{w^{l}}{1-\widetilde{q}^{l}}.

It follows that (
A.5
) holds up to addition of a term which is
constant with respect to
w
. In order to fix this freedom, it
suffices to note that (
A.5
) holds for
w=0
.

Using the previous identities, we obtain

W_{\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)-\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)={\rm i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}.

Now notice that under our assumptions on
t
and
\epsilon
, we have
W_{\rho_{0}}=W_{{\rm np}}
by Proposition
A.5
. We now show that
W_{{\rm np}}
admits the following representation as sum over residues:

W_{{\rm np}}(\epsilon,t)=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}tk}}{({\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}k\epsilon}-1)}.

In order to see this, let us recall that by definition we have

\displaystyle W_{\rm np}(\epsilon,t)=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{({\rm e}^{s}-1)({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)}.

The integrand has two series of poles, one
at
u=2\pi\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\check{\epsilon}}l
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}
and the other at

u=2\pi\mathrm{i}k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. We can compute the previous integral by closing the contour in the upper
half-plane. The contributions from the poles at
u=2\pi\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\check{\epsilon}}l
are calculated as

\displaystyle\frac{(2\pi)^{2}\mathrm{i}}{\check{\epsilon}}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{s^{2}({\rm e}^{s}-1)}\Big|_{s=2\pi\mathrm{i}l/{\check{\epsilon}}}=\frac{(2\pi)^{2}\mathrm{i}}{\check{\epsilon}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}lt/\check{\epsilon}}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i}l/\check{\epsilon})^{2}({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}l/\check{\epsilon}}-1)}=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\frac{w^{l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}

while the contributions of the poles at
u=2\pi\mathrm{i}k
gives

(2\pi)^{2}\mathrm{i}\frac{{\rm e}^{st}}{s^{2}({\rm e}^{\check{\epsilon}s}-1)}\Big|_{s=2\pi\mathrm{i}k}=(2\pi)^{2}\mathrm{i}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kt}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i}k)^{2}({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k\check{\epsilon}}-1)}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}kt}}{k^{2}({\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}k\epsilon}-1)}.

In particular, we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}(W_{\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)-W_{\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t))+W_{\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}+\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}tk}}{({\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}k\epsilon}-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}tk}}{({\rm e}^{\mathrm{i}k\epsilon}-1)}=W(\epsilon,t).

Now we prove the other limit. Using the jumps along the Stokes rays
-l_{k}
for
k<0
, we find that

\displaystyle W_{-\rho_{0}}-\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=-1}^{-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k+1}}-W_{-\rho_{k}}=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k=-1}^{-\infty}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}(t+k)/\check{\epsilon}}\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w^{-1}\widetilde{q}^{k}\big)+\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w^{-1}\big).

Using the constraints on
t
and
\epsilon
, we find that
|w^{-1}|<1
and
|\widetilde{q}|<1
, so that we can expand in series as in Proposition
5.15
and write

W_{-\rho_{0}}-\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}=-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}+\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w^{-1}\big).

On the other hand, using the relation
W_{-\rho}(\epsilon,t)=-W_{\rho}(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)
, we find that

W_{-\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)=-W_{\rho_{0}}(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)=-W_{\rm np}(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q).

Using the same argument as before with the integral representation of
W_{\rm np}
, we find that

\displaystyle W_{-\rho_{0}}(\epsilon,t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{-l})}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}tk}}{({\rm e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\epsilon}-1)}+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{-l})}-W(-\epsilon,t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(Q)=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{-l})}+W(\epsilon,t).

Joining our results together, we conclude that

\begin{split}\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)&=\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{-l})}\bigg)+\bigg(\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}\bigg)\bigg)+W(\epsilon,t)-\mathrm{i}\check{\epsilon}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w^{-1}\big).\end{split}

Finally, notice that

\begin{split}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{-l})}\bigg)+\bigg(\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}(1-\widetilde{q}^{l})}\bigg)\bigg)&=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{-l}}{l^{2}}=\mathrm{Li}_{2}\big(w^{-1}\big),\end{split}

where in the last equality we used that
\big|w^{-1}\big|<1
. Hence, we conclude that

\lim_{k\to-\infty}W_{-\rho_{k}}(\epsilon,t)=W(\epsilon,t).

∎

Appendix B
Special functions

B.1
Multiple gamma and multiple sine functions

Here we introduce the building blocks for the solutions of the refined difference equation. We will follow the exposition and notation of
[
83
]
.

Suppose
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}\in\mathbb{C}
all lie on the same side of some straight line through the origin, then the multiple zeta function is defined by the series

\zeta_{r}(s,z\mid\underline{\omega})=\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{r}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n_{1}\omega_{1}+\dots+n_{r}\omega_{r}+z)^{s}},

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\operatorname{Re}(s)>r
, where the exponential is rendered one-valued. This series is holomorphic in the domain
\{\operatorname{Re}(s)>r\}
and is analytically continued to
s\in\mathbb{C}
.

Since it is holomorphic at
s=0
we can define the multiple gamma function by

\Gamma_{r}(z|\underline{\omega})=\exp\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\zeta_{r}(s,z\mid\underline{\omega})|_{s=0}\right),

where

|\underline{\omega}|=\omega_{1}+\dots+\omega_{r},

and the multiple sine function is given by

\sin_{r}(z\mid\underline{\omega})=\Gamma_{r}(z\mid\underline{\omega})^{-1}\Gamma_{r}(|\underline{\omega}|-z\mid\underline{\omega})^{(-1)^{r}}.

This satisfies the functional equations

\displaystyle\sin_{r}(z+\omega_{j}\mid\underline{\omega})=\sin_{r-1}\big(z\mid\underline{\omega}^{-}(j)\big)^{-1}\sin_{r}(z\mid\underline{\omega}),

\displaystyle\sin_{r}(z\mid\underline{\omega})\sin_{r}(|\underline{\omega}|-z\mid\underline{\omega})^{(-1)^{r}}=1,

(B.1)

where

\underline{\omega}^{-}(j)=\big(\omega_{1},\dots,\overset{\vee}{\omega_{j}},\dots,\omega_{r}\big),

and
\overset{\vee}{\omega_{j}}
means omitting
\omega_{j}
.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
\underline{\omega}=(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})
,
\omega_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
we define the multiple Bernoulli polynomials
B_{r,n}(z,\underline{\omega})
in terms of the generating function

\mathcal{B}_{r}(z,x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})=\frac{x^{r}{\rm e}^{zx}}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}({\rm e}^{\omega_{j}x}-1)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}B_{r,n}(z|\underline{\omega})\frac{x^{n}}{n!}.

(B.2)

Proposition B.1
.

The generating function of the Bernoulli polynomials
B_{r,n}(z\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})
obeys

\mathcal{B}_{r}(z+\omega_{j},x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})-\mathcal{B}_{r}(z,x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})=x\cdot\mathcal{B}_{r-1}(z,x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\overset{\vee}{\omega_{j}},\dots,\omega_{r})

where
\overset{\vee}{\omega_{j}}
means omitting
\omega_{j}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately by using the definition (
B.2
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{r}(z+\omega_{j},x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})-\mathcal{B}_{r}(z,x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{x\cdot.x^{r-1}{\rm e}^{zx}({\rm e}^{\omega_{j}x}-1)}{\prod_{i=1}^{r}({\rm e}^{\omega_{i}x}-1)}

\displaystyle=x\cdot\mathcal{B}_{r-1}\big(z,x\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\overset{\vee}{\omega_{j}},\dots,\omega_{r}\big).

∎

This proposition implies in particular that

B_{3,3}(z+\omega_{j}\mid\underline{\omega})-B_{3,3}(z|\underline{\omega})=3\cdot B_{2,2}(z\mid\underline{\omega}^{-}(j)),

(B.3)

where
\underline{\omega}=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})
and where
\underline{\omega}^{-}(j)=\big(\omega_{1},\dots,\overset{\vee}{\omega_{j}},\dots,\omega_{r}\big)
.

Next, we discuss a few special functions that play an important role in this paper.

The multiple sine functions are defined using the Barnes multiple Gamma functions
[
16
]
. For a variable
z\in\mathbb{C}
and parameters
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
these are defined by

\sin_{r}(z\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}):=\Gamma_{r}(z\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})^{-1}\cdot\Gamma_{r}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}-z\mid\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}\right)^{(-1)^{r}}.

For further definitions see, e.g.,
[
21
,
92
]
and references therein.

Another special function is (related to) Fadeev’s quantum dilogarithm

\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2}):=\exp\left(-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}\cdot B_{2,2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\right)\cdot\sin_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2}),

which we have introduced in terms of the double sine function as in
[
21
]
.

The final special function is defined by
16
16

16

In
[
21
]
,
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is called
F
and
\mathcal{S}_{3}
is called
G
, we changed the label and introduced the subscripts to avoid confusion with the free energies and to make the relation to the double and triple sine functions clear.

\mathcal{S}_{3}(z|\omega_{1},\omega_{2}):=\exp\left(\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{6}\cdot B_{3,3}(z+\omega_{1}\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\right)\cdot\sin_{3}(z+\omega_{1}\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}).

B.2
Quantum dilogarithm and its properties

In this section we summarize a few properties of the function
\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
, that is a single-valued meromorphic function of variables
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, following
[
21
, Section 4]
and
[
83
]
. Say,

\displaystyle x_{1}=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}z/\omega_{1}),\qquad

\displaystyle x_{2}=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}z/\omega_{2}),

\displaystyle q_{1}=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}),\qquad

\displaystyle q_{2}=\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}\omega_{1}/\omega_{2}).

Under the assumption
\omega_{1}/\omega_{2}\notin\mathbb{R}_{<0}
, the function
\mathcal{S}_{2}
has the following properties:

1.

It is regular and non-vanishing except at the points

z=a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2},\qquad a,b\in\mathbb{Z},

which are zeroes if
a,b\leq 0
, poles if
a,b>0
, and otherwise neither.

2.

It is symmetric in the arguments
\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}
:

\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{2},\omega_{1}),

and is invariant under simultaneous rescaling of all three arguments.

3.

It satisfies the two difference relations:

\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(z+\omega_{1}\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}=\frac{1}{1-x_{2}},\qquad\frac{\mathcal{S}_{2}(z+\omega_{2}\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}=\frac{1}{1-x_{1}}.

(B.4)

4.

It has the product expansion

\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\prod_{k\geq 1}\big(1-x_{1}q_{1}^{-k}\big)^{-1}\cdot\prod_{k\geq 0}\big(1-x_{2}q_{2}^{k}\big),

(B.5)

valid when
\operatorname{Im}(\omega_{1}/\omega_{2})>0.

5.

When
\operatorname{Re}(\omega_{i})>0
and
0<\operatorname{Re}(z)<\operatorname{Re}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})
it has the integral representation:

\mathcal{S}_{2}(z|\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\exp\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm e}^{zs}}{({\rm e}^{\omega_{1}s}-1)({\rm e}^{\omega_{2}s}-1)}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s}\right),

(B.6)

where the contour
\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}
follows the real axis from
-\infty
to
+\infty
avoiding the origin by a small detour in the upper half-plane.

6.

Fix
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
with
0<\operatorname{Re}(z)<\operatorname{Re}(\omega_{2})
and
\operatorname{Im}(z/\omega_{2})>0
. Then it has the asymptotic expansion:

\log\mathcal{S}_{2}(z\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\sim\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{B_{k}\cdot\omega_{1}^{k-1}}{k!}\cdot\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{\omega_{2}}\right)^{k-1}\cdot{\rm Li}_{2-k}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}z/\omega_{2}}\big),

(B.7)

valid as
\omega_{1}\rightarrow 0
in any closed subsector
\Sigma
of the half-plane
\operatorname{Re}(\omega_{1})>0
.

B.2.1
Relation to Faddeev’s quantum dilogarithm

Faddeev’s quantum dilogarithm
\Phi_{b}(z)
, which is defined by

\Phi_{b}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zs}}{4\sinh(sb)\sinh(sb^{-1})}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s}\right).

The function
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is related to Faddeev’s quantum dilogarithm
as

\Phi_{b}(z)=\mathcal{S}_{2}\bigl(-\mathrm{i}z+\big(b+b^{-1}\big)/2\mid b,b^{-1}\bigr).

B.2.2
Special values

Using the integral representation of the quantum dilogarithm we can find the expression for the quantum dilogarithm for
\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}
. We find

\displaystyle\log\left(\mathcal{S}_{2}(z+\omega_{1}\mid\omega_{1},\omega_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}+\mathrm{i}0^{+}}\frac{{\rm e}^{(z+\omega_{1})s}}{({\rm e}^{\omega_{1}s}-1)({\rm e}^{\omega_{1}s}-1)}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega_{1}}\left(\frac{\omega_{1}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\cdot{\rm Li}_{2}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}z/\omega_{1}}\big)\right).

(B.8)

The proof of this equation can be found for instance in the proof of Proposition 4.4 in
[
21
]
.

B.3
Product forms

See
[
83
, Proposition 5]
. Let
r\geq 2
and
\operatorname{Im}(\omega_{j}/\omega_{k})\neq 0
, then the multiple sine function
\sin_{r}(z|\underline{\omega})
has the following infinite product representation:

\displaystyle\sin_{r}(z\mid\underline{\omega})

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{(-1)^{r}\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{r}B_{rr}(z\mid\underline{\omega})\right\}\prod_{k=1}^{r}(x_{k};\underline{q_{k}})_{\infty}^{(r-2)}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{(-1)^{r-1}\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{r}B_{rr}(z\mid\underline{\omega})\right\}\prod_{k=1}^{r}\big(x_{k}^{-1};\underline{q_{k}}^{-1}\big)_{\infty}^{(r-2)},

(B.9)

where we have set

\displaystyle x_{k}={\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}z/\omega_{k}},\qquad q_{jk}={\rm e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\omega_{j}/\omega_{k}},

\displaystyle\underline{q_{k}}=\big(q_{1k},\dots,\overset{\vee}{q_{kk}},\dots,q_{rk}\big),\qquad\underline{q_{k}}^{-1}=\big(q_{1k}^{-1},\dots,\overset{\vee}{q_{kk}^{-1}},\dots,q_{rk}^{-1}\big),

and where

(x;\underline{q})_{\infty}^{(r)}=\prod_{j_{0},\dots,j_{r}=0}^{\infty}\big(1-xq_{0}^{j_{0}}\cdots q_{r}^{j_{r}}\big),

with
\underline{q}=(q_{0},\dots,q_{r})
.

See
[
83
, Proposition 1]
. It is also useful to know that

(x;\underline{q})_{\infty}^{(r)}=\frac{1}{\big(q_{j}^{-1}x;\underline{q}[j]\big)_{\infty}^{(r)}},

(B.10)

where

\underline{q}[j]=\big(q_{0},\dots,q_{j}^{-1},\dots,q_{r}\big)
.
